.
∎

VI
Büchi: visiting targets repeatedly

This section solves the almost-sure Büchi problem. As before, for the rest of
this section we fix a CMDP
\mathcal{C}=(S,A,\Delta,\gamma,\mathit{R},\mathit{cap})
and a set
T\subseteq S
.

The solution builds on the positive reachability problem similarly to how the
safety problem builds on the nonreloading reachability problem. In particular,
we identify a largest set
\mathit{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{R}
such that from each

r\in\mathit{R}^{\prime}
we can safely reach
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
again (in at least one step)
while restricting ourselves only to safe strategies that (i) avoid

\mathit{R}\setminus\mathit{R}^{\prime}
and (ii) guarantee positive reachability of

T
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})
from all
r\in\mathit{R}^{\prime}
.

Intuitively, at each visit of
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
, such a strategy can attempt to
reach
T
. With an infinite number of attempts, we reach
T

infinitely often with probability 1 (almost surely). Formally, we show that
for a suitable
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
we have that
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}^{>0}
(where
\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})

denotes the CMDP defined as
\mathcal{C}
with the exception that
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
is the
set of reload states).

Algorithm
4
identifies the suitable set
\mathit{R}^{\prime}
using

Algorithm

3
in a similar fashion as
Algorithm

2
handled safety
using
Algorithm

1
. In each iteration, we declare as
non-reload states all states of
\mathit{R}
from which positive reachability of

T
within
\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})
cannot be guaranteed. This is repeated until
we reach a fixed point. The number of iterations is clearly bounded by

|\mathit{R}|
.

Input:

CMDP
\mathcal{C}=(S,A,\Delta,\gamma,\mathit{R},\mathit{cap})
and
T\subseteq S

Output:

The vector
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}
, rule selector

\Phi

\mathit{R}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathit{R}
;
\mathit{Unusable}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

1

repeat

2

\mathit{R}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathit{R}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\mathit{Unusable}
;

3

/*
The next 2 lines use
Algorithm

3
on
\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})
and
T
. */

\mathbf{b}\leftarrow\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}^{>0}
;

4

\Phi\leftarrow
witness selector for
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}^{>0}
;

5

\mathit{Unusable}\leftarrow\{r\in\mathit{R}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{b}(r)>\mathit{cap}\}
;

6

until

\mathit{Unusable}=\emptyset
;

7

return

\mathbf{b},\Phi

Algorithm 4

Computing
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{T}]^{=1}
and a corresponding witness rule selector.

Theorem 7
.

Upon termination of
Algorithm

4
we have that for the strategy

\sigma_{\Phi}
encoded by
\Phi
it holds

\sigma_{\Phi}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-8.29843pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-5.56233pt\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.24844pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-4.51234pt\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}\!\mathsf{B}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}
. As a consequence, a polynomial-size finite counter
strategy for the almost-sure Büchi problem can be computed in time polynomial
with respect to the representation of
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The complexity part follows from the fact that the number of iterations of the
repeat-loop is bounded by
|\mathit{R}|
and from
theorems
5
and
6
.

For the correctness part, we first prove that

\sigma_{\Phi}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-8.29843pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-5.56233pt\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.24844pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-4.51234pt\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}\!\mathsf{B}
. Then we
argue that the same holds also for
\mathcal{C}
. Finally, we show that
\mathbf{b}\leq\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}
; the converse inequality follows from

\sigma_{\Phi}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-8.29843pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-5.56233pt\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.24844pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-4.51234pt\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}\!\mathsf{B}
.

Strategy
\sigma_{\Phi}
has finite memory, also

\sigma_{\Phi}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-8.29843pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-5.56233pt\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.24844pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-4.51234pt\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}\!\mathsf{R}
, and
upon termination,
\mathbf{b}(r)=0
for all
r\in\mathit{R}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, there is
\theta>0
such that upon every visit of some state
r\in\mathit{R}^{\prime}
we have that
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{0}}}_{{{r}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-7.93613pt{0}\kern 5.37502pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-7.83292pt{r}\kern 5.37502pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{0}}}_{{{r}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-7.93613pt{0}\kern 5.37502pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-7.83292pt{r}\kern 5.37502pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{0}}}_{{{r}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-5.19583pt{0}\kern 3.33472pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-5.1221pt{r}\kern 3.33472pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{0}}}_{{{r}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-4.24306pt{0}\kern 2.38194pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-4.16933pt{r}\kern 2.38194pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}(\mathsf{R})\geq\theta
.

As
\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})
is decreasing, every safe infinite run created by

\sigma_{\Phi}
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})
must visit
\mathit{R}^{\prime}

infinitely many times. Hence, with probability 1 we reach
T
at least
once. The argument can then be repeated from the first point of visit of

T
to show that with probability 1 we visit
T
at least twice,
three times, etc.
ad infinitum.
By the monotonicity of probability, we
get
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-8.03651pt{d}\kern 5.37502pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-7.78299pt{s}\kern 5.37502pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-8.03651pt{d}\kern 5.37502pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-7.78299pt{s}\kern 5.37502pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-5.26753pt{d}\kern 3.33472pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-5.08646pt{s}\kern 3.33472pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-4.31476pt{d}\kern 2.38194pt{\sigma_{\Phi}}}}_{{\kern-4.13368pt{s}\kern 2.38194pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}}}}(\mathsf{B})=1
for
all
s
with
d=\mathbf{b}(s)\leq\mathit{cap}
and

\sigma_{\Phi}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-8.29843pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-5.56233pt\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.24844pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-4.51234pt\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime}}}}}\!\mathsf{B}
.

Now we show that also
\sigma_{\Phi}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.76787pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.13734pt\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-8.29843pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-5.56233pt\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathbf{b}}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.24844pt{\mathbf{b}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-4.51234pt\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}\!\mathsf{B}
.
Let
s
be a state such that
\mathbf{b}(s)\leq\mathit{cap}
. Clearly, all

s
-initiated runs loaded by
d\geq\mathbf{b}(s)
that are compatible
with
\sigma_{\Phi}
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})
avoid

\mathit{R}\smallsetminus\mathit{R}^{\prime}
. Therefore,

\mathsf{Comp}_{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma_{\Phi},s,d)=\mathsf{Comp}_{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}(\sigma_{\Phi},s,d)
and we also get

\sigma_{\Phi}\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.88403pt{d}\kern 5.95122pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.70296pt{s}\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-6.83405pt{d}\kern 4.90123pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-6.65297pt{s}\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}\!\mathsf{B}
.

It remains to show that
\mathbf{b}\leq\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}
. Assume for the
sake of contradiction that there is a state
s\in S
and a strategy

\sigma
such that
\sigma\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.88403pt{d}\kern 5.95122pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-7.70296pt{s}\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-6.83405pt{d}\kern 4.90123pt{\!=1}}}_{{\kern-6.65297pt{s}\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}\!\mathsf{B}
for some

d<\mathbf{b}(s)=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]_{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}^{>0}(s)
. Then there must be at
least one
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-5.06735pt{d}\kern 3.13454pt}}_{{\kern-9.07509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.13454pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-4.17177pt{d}\kern 2.23895pt}}_{{\kern-8.1795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.23895pt}}}
created by
\sigma
such that

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-5.06735pt{d}\kern 3.13454pt}}_{{\kern-9.07509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.13454pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-4.17177pt{d}\kern 2.23895pt}}_{{\kern-8.1795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.23895pt}}}
visits
r\in\mathit{R}\setminus\mathit{R}^{\prime}
before
reaching
T
(otherwise
d\geq\mathbf{b}(s)
). Then either (a)

\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{>0}(r)=\infty
, in which case any
\sigma
-compatible extension of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-5.06735pt{d}\kern 3.13454pt}}_{{\kern-9.07509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.13454pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-4.17177pt{d}\kern 2.23895pt}}_{{\kern-8.1795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.23895pt}}}
avoids
T
; or
(b) since
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]_{\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime})}^{>0}(r)>\mathit{cap}
,
there must be an extension of
\alpha
that visits, between the visit of
r

and
T
, another
r^{\prime}\in\mathit{R}\setminus\mathit{R}^{\prime}
such
that
r^{\prime}\neq r
. We can then repeat the argument, eventually reaching
the case (a) or running out of the resource, a contradiction with

\sigma\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.88403pt{d}\kern 5.95122pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-7.70296pt{s}\kern 5.95122pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-6.83405pt{d}\kern 4.90123pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-6.65297pt{s}\kern 4.90123pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}\!\mathsf{S}
.∎

VII
Almost-sure reachability

In this section, we solve the
almost-sure reachability
problem, which
is computing the vector
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]^{=1}
and the
corresponding witness strategy for a given set of target states

T\subseteq S
.

VII-A

Reduction to Büchi

In the absence of the resource constraints, reachability can be viewed as a special case of Büchi: we can simply modify the MDP so that playing any action in some target state
t\in T
results into looping in
t
, thus replacing reachability with an equivalent Büchi condition. In consumption MDPs, the transformation is slightly more involved, due to the need to “survive” after reaching
T
. Hence, for every CMDP
\mathcal{C}
and a target set
T
we define a new CMDP
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
so that solving
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
w.r.t. the Büchi objective entails solving
\mathcal{C}
w.r.t. the reachability objective. Formally, for
\mathcal{C}=(S,A,\Delta,\gamma,\mathit{R},\mathit{cap})
we have
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)=(S^{\prime},A,\Delta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\mathit{R}^{\prime},\mathit{cap})
, where the differing components are defined as follows:

•

S^{\prime}=S\cup\{\mathit{sink}\}
, where
\mathit{sink}\not\in S
is a new sink state, i.e.
\Delta^{\prime}(\mathit{sink},a,\mathit{sink})=1
for each
a\in A
;

•

we have
\mathit{R}^{\prime}=\mathit{R}\cup\{\mathit{sink}\}
;

•

for each
t\in T
and
a\in A
we have
\Delta^{\prime}(t,a,\mathit{sink})=1
and
\Delta^{\prime}(t,a,s)=0
for all
s\in S
;

•

for each
t\in T
and
a\in A
we have
\gamma^{\prime}(t,a)=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}}(t)
;

•

we have
\gamma^{\prime}(\mathit{sink},a)=1
for each
a\in A
; and

•

we have
\Delta^{\prime}(s,a,t)=\Delta(s,a,t)
and
\gamma^{\prime}(s,a)=\gamma(s,a)
for every
s\in S\setminus T
, every
a\in A
, and every
t\in S
.

We can easily prove the following:

Lemma 5
.

For every
s\in S
it holds that
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}(s)=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{\{\mathit{sink}\}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)}^{=1}(s)
. Moreover, from a
witness strategy for
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}
we can
extract, in time polynomial with respect to the representation of
\mathcal{C}
, the
witness strategy for
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{\{\mathit{sink}\}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)}^{=1}

and vice versa.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be a witness strategy for
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{\{\mathit{sink}\}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)}^{=1}
in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
. Consider a strategy
\pi
in
\mathcal{C}
which, starting in some state
s
, mimics
\sigma
until some
t\in T
is reached (this is possible since
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
only differs from
\mathcal{C}
on
T\cup\{\mathit{sink}\}
), and upon reaching
T
switches to mimicking an arbitrary safe strategy. Since
\sigma
reaches
\mathit{sink}
and thus also
T
almost-surely, so does
\pi
. Moreover, since
\sigma
is safe, upon reaching a
t\in T
the current resource level is at least
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{{\mathcal{C}}{}}(t)
, since consuming this amount is enforced in the next step. This is sufficient for
\pi
to prevent resource exhaustion after switching to a safe strategy.

It follows that
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{\{\mathit{sink}\}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)}^{=1}(s)\geq\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}(s)
for all
s\in S.
The converse inequality can be proved similarly, by defining a straightforward conversion of a witness strategy for
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}
into a witness strategy for
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{B}_{\{\mathit{sink}\}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)}^{=1}(s)
. The conversion can be clearly performed in polynomial time, with the help of Algorithm
2
.
∎

Hence, we can solve almost-sure reachability for
\mathcal{C}
by constructing
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
and solving the latter for almost-sure Büchi via
Algorithm
4
. The construction of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)

can be clearly performed in time polynomial in the representation of
\mathcal{C}

(using Algorithm
2
to compute
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}}
), hence
also almost-sure reachability can be solved in polynomial time.

VII-B

Almost-sure reachability without model modification

In practice, building
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
and translating the synthesized
strategy back to
\mathcal{C}
is inconvenient. Hence, we also present an algorithm
to solve almost-sure reachability directly on
\mathcal{C}
. The algorithm consists
of a minor modification of the already presented algorithms.

To argue the correctness of the algorithm, we need a slight generalization of the MDP modification. We call a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\overline{\mathbb{N}}^{S}
a
sink vector
for
\mathcal{C}
if and only
if
0\leq\mathbf{v}(s)\leq\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}(s)
or

\mathbf{v}(s)=\infty
for all
s\in S
. By

F(\mathbf{v})
we denote the set
\{s\in S\mid\mathbf{v}(s)<\infty\}
of states with finite value of
\mathbf{v}
and
we call each member of this set a
sink entry
. We say that
\mathbf{v}
is a sink vector
for
T
if
F(\mathbf{v})=T
. Given a CMDP
\mathcal{C}
, target set
T
, and sink vector
\mathbf{v}
for
T
, we define a new CMDP
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{v})
in exactly the same way as
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
, except for the fourth point: for every
t\in T
we put
\gamma^{\prime}(t,a)=\mathbf{v}(t)
for all
a\in A
. Note that
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}})
.

Input:

CMDP
\mathcal{C}=(S,A,\Delta,\gamma,\mathit{R},\mathit{cap})
, sets of states
T,X\subseteq S,
a sink vector
\mathbf{v}\in\overline{\mathbb{N}}^{S}
for
T

Output:

\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{N}_{+X\cup\{\mathit{sink}\}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{v})}
projected to
S

\mathbf{x}\leftarrow\mathbf{\infty}^{S}
;

1

repeat

2

\mathbf{x}_{\mathit{old}}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}
;

3

foreach

s\in S

do

4

if

s\in F(\mathbf{v})

then

5

\mathbf{x}(s)\leftarrow\mathbf{v}(s)
;

6

else

7

c\leftarrow\min_{a\in A}\mathit{AV}(\llfloor\,\mathbf{x}_{\mathit{old}}\,\rrfloor_{X},s,a)
;

8

if

c<\mathbf{x}(s)

then

9

\mathbf{x}(s)\leftarrow c
;

10

until

\,\mathbf{x}_{\mathit{old}}=\mathbf{x}
;

11

return

\mathbf{x}

Algorithm 5

Modified safe sure reachability.

Given a CMDP
\mathcal{C}
, subsets of states
T,X
of
\mathcal{C}
, and a sink vector
\mathbf{v}
for
T
, Algorithm
5
computes the
S
-components of the vector
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{N}_{+X^{\prime}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{v})}
, where
X^{\prime}=X\cup\{\mathit{sink}\}
. To see this, denote by
\mathbf{x}_{i}
the contents of the variable
\mathbf{x}
in the
i
-th iteration of Algorithm
5
on the input
\mathcal{C}
,
T
,
X
,
\mathbf{v}
; and by
\mathbf{v}_{i}
the contents of variable
\mathbf{v}
in the
i
-th iteration of an execution of Algorithm
1
on CMDP
{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{v})}
with target set
X\cup\{\mathit{sink}\}
. A straightforward induction shows that
\mathbf{v}_{i}(s)=\mathbf{x}_{i}(s)
for all
s\in S
and all
i
.

Then, the vector
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}{}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{v})}
can be computed using a
slight modification of
Algorithm

2
: on
Algorithm

2
use

\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{N}_{+R^{\prime}\cup\{\mathit{sink}\}}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{v})}
(projected to
S
) computed by

Algorithm

5
instead of
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{N}_{+\mathit{R}^{\prime}}]
computed by
Algorithm

1
. Then, run the modified algorithm on
\mathcal{C}
. The correctness can be argued similarly as for Algorithm
5
: let
R^{\prime}_{i}
be the contents of
R^{\prime}
in the
i
-th iteration of Algorithm
2
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T,\mathbf{v})
; and let
\tilde{R}^{\prime}_{i}
be the contents of
R^{\prime}
in the
i
-th iteration of the modified algorithm executed on
\mathcal{C}
. Clearly,
\mathit{sink}\in\tilde{R}^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
. An induction on
i
shows that for all
i
we have
R^{\prime}=\tilde{R}^{\prime}\setminus\{\mathit{sink}\}
, so both algorithms terminate in the same iteration. The correctness follows from the correctness of Algorithm
2
.

Now we can proceed to solve almost-sure reachability. Algorithm
6
combines (slightly modified) algorithms
3
and
4
to mimic the solving of Büchi objective in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
with a single Büchi accepting state
\{\mathit{sink}\}
.

Lines
6
-
6
correspond to the computation of Algorithm
3

on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)(\mathit{R}^{\prime}\cup\{\mathit{sink}\})
. To see this, note that
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)(\mathit{R}^{\prime}\cup\{\mathit{sink}\})=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime}),T,\mathbf{v})
for
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}}
. Hence, line
3
in Algorithm 3 is replaced by line
6
in

Algorithm

6
. Also, on lines
6
and
6
(which correspond to
lines
3
and
3
in Algorithm
3
), we use generalized versions
of
\mathit{HV}
and
\mathit{SV}
that use
alternative
survival values

\mathbf{s}
:

\displaystyle\mathit{HV}[\mathbf{s}](\mathbf{x},s,a,s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)\\
t\neq s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\{\mathbf{x}(s^{\prime}),\mathbf{s}(t)\}

\displaystyle\mathit{SV}[\mathbf{s}](\mathbf{x},s,a)

\displaystyle=\gamma(s,a)+\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)}\mathit{HV}[\mathbf{s}](\mathbf{x},s,a,s^{\prime})

Note that
\mathit{HV}=\mathit{HV}[\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]]
and
\mathit{SV}=\mathit{SV}[\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]]
. These generalized operators are used because

Algorithm

6
works on
\mathcal{C}
,
but lines
6
and
6
should emulate the computation of lines

3
and
3
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)(\mathit{R}^{\prime}\cup\{\mathit{sink}\})
, so the vector

\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}}
in the definition of the hope value
\mathit{HV}
has to be substituted for

\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)(\mathit{R}^{\prime}\cup\{\mathit{sink}\})}=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}(\mathit{R}^{\prime}),T,\mathbf{v})}
where
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Input:

CMDP
\mathcal{C}=(S,A,\Delta,\gamma,\mathit{R},\mathit{cap})
and

T\subseteq S

Output:

The vector
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]_{\mathcal{C}}^{=1}
, rule selector

\Phi

\mathbf{o}\leftarrow\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{C}}
;

/*
Algorithm

2
*/

\mathbf{v}\leftarrow\{\infty\}^{S}
;

1

foreach

t\in T

do

\mathbf{v}(t)\leftarrow\mathbf{o}(t)
;

2

\mathit{R}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathit{R}
;
\mathit{Unusable}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

3

repeat

4

\mathit{R}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathit{R}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\mathit{Unusable}
;

5

\Phi\leftarrow\text{ an empty selector}
;

6

foreach

s\in S

do

7

\Phi(s)(\mathbf{o}(s))\leftarrow\text{arbitrary min-safe{} action of }s

\mathbf{p}\leftarrow\mathbf{v}
;

8

\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}]_{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}(R^{\prime}),T,\mathbf{v})}
;

/*
modified
alg.
2
*/

repeat

9

\mathbf{p}_{\mathit{old}}\leftarrow\mathbf{p}
;

10

foreach

s\in S\smallsetminus T

do

11

\mathbf{a}(s)\leftarrow\arg\min_{a\in A}\mathit{SV}[\mathbf{s}](\mathbf{p}_{\mathit{old}},s,a)
;

12

\mathbf{p}(s)\leftarrow\min_{a\in A}\mathit{SV}[\mathbf{s}](\mathbf{p}_{\mathit{old}},s,a)
;

13

\mathbf{p}\leftarrow\llbracket\,\mathbf{p}\,\rrbracket_{\mathit{R}^{\prime}}^{\mathit{cap}}
;

14

foreach

s\in S\smallsetminus T

do

15

if

\mathbf{p}(s)<\mathbf{p}_{\mathit{old}}(s)

then

16

\Phi(s)(\mathbf{p}(s))\leftarrow\mathbf{a}(s)
;

17

until

\,\mathbf{p}_{\mathit{old}}=\mathbf{p}
;

18

\mathit{Unusable}\leftarrow\{r\in\mathit{R}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{p}(r)>\mathit{cap}\}
;

19

until

\mathit{Unusable}=\emptyset
;

20

return

\mathbf{p},\Phi

Algorithm 6

Computing
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}_{T}]^{=1}
and a corresponding witness rule selector.

Hence, the respective lines indeed emulate the computation of
Algorithm

3
on

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)(\mathit{R}^{\prime}\cup\{\mathit{sink}\})
. It remains to show that the whole repeat loop emulates the
computation of
Algorithm

4
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C},T)
. But this follows immediately from the fact that in
the latter computation,
\mathit{sink}
always stays in
R^{\prime}
.

VIII
Improving Expected Reachability Time

The number of steps that a strategy needs on average to reach the target set

T
(
expected reachability time (ERT)
) is a property of practical
importance. For example, we expect that a patrolling unmanned vehicle visits all the
checkpoints in a reasonable amount of time. The presented approach is purely qualitative (ensures reachability with
probability 1) and thus does not consider the number of steps at all. In this
section, we introduce two heuristics that can improve ERT: the

goal-leaning
heuristic and the
threshold
heuristic. These slight
modifications of the algorithms produce strategies that can often hit

T
sooner than the strategies produced by the unmodified algorithms.

VIII-A

Expected reachability time

To formally define ERT, we introduce a new objective:
\mathsf{F}_{\!T}^{i}

(
reachability first in
i
steps
) as
\mathsf{F}_{\!T}^{i}=\{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathit{\varrho}^{{\kern-6.81049pt{d}\kern 4.14899pt}}_{{\kern-12.42133pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.80954pt}}}\notin\mathsf{R}_{T}^{j}\text{ for all }0\leq j<i\}
(the set of all safe loaded runs
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathit{\varrho}^{{\kern-6.81049pt{d}\kern 4.14899pt}}_{{\kern-12.42133pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.14899pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathit{\varrho}^{{\kern-6.81049pt{d}\kern 4.14899pt}}_{{\kern-12.42133pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.14899pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathit{\varrho}^{{\kern-4.46619pt{d}\kern 2.53337pt}}_{{\kern-8.47392pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.53337pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathit{\varrho}^{{\kern-3.74236pt{d}\kern 1.80954pt}}_{{\kern-7.75009pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.80954pt}}}
such that the minimum

j
such that
\mathit{\varrho}_{j}\in T
is equal to
i
). Finally, the
expected
reachability time
for a a strategy
\sigma
, an initial state
s\in S
,
an initial load
d\leq\mathit{cap}
, and a target set
T\subseteq S
is
defined as follows.

\mathit{ERT}_{\mathcal{C}}(\sigma,s,d,T)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}i\cdot\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-8.03651pt{d}\kern 5.37502pt{\sigma}}}_{{\kern-7.78299pt{s}\kern 5.37502pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-8.03651pt{d}\kern 5.37502pt{\sigma}}}_{{\kern-7.78299pt{s}\kern 5.37502pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-5.26753pt{d}\kern 3.33472pt{\sigma}}}_{{\kern-5.08646pt{s}\kern 3.33472pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\mathbb{P}^{{\kern-4.31476pt{d}\kern 2.38194pt{\sigma}}}_{{\kern-4.13368pt{s}\kern 2.38194pt{\mathcal{C}}}}}(\mathsf{F}_{\!T}^{i})

.

, we have

and

\mathit{ERT}(\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}},s,2,\{t\})=20
. Indeed,

.

VIII-B

Goal-leaning heuristic

Actions to play in certain states with a particular amount of resource are
selected on
Algorithm

3
of
Algorithm

3
(and on

Algorithm

6
of
Algorithm

6
) based on the actions’

save values
\mathit{SV}
. This value in
s
based on

\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{=1}
is equal to 2 for both actions
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}
.
Thus,
Algorithm

3
(and also
Algorithm

6
) returns

\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}}
or
\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}}
randomly based on the resolution of the

\arg\min
operator on
Algorithm

3
(
Algorithm

6
in

Algorithm

6
) for
s
. The
goal-leaning heuristic
fixes the
resolution of the
\arg\min
operator to always pick
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}
in this example.

The ordinary
\arg\min
operator selects randomly an action from the pool of
actions with the minimal value
v_{\min}
of the function
\mathit{SV}
for
s

(and the current values of
\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}}
). Loosely speaking, the goal-leaning

\arg\min
operator chooses, instead, the action whose chance to reach the
desired successor used to obtain
v_{\min}
is maximal among actions in this
pool.

The value
\mathit{SV}
is computed using successors’ hope values (
\mathit{HV}
),
see
Section

V
. The goal-leaning
\arg\min
operator records, when
computing the
\mathit{HV}
values, also the transition probabilities of the
desired successors. Let
s
be a state, let
a
be an action, and let

t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)
be the successor of
a
in
s
that minimizes

\mathit{HV}(\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}},s,a,t)

and
maximizes
\Delta(s,a,t)
(in this
order). We denote by
p_{s,a}
the value
\Delta(s,a,t)
. The goal-leaning

\arg\min
operator chooses the action
a
in
s
that minimizes

\mathit{SV}(\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}},s,a)

and
maximizes
p_{s,a}
.

In the example from
Fig.

5
we have that
p_{s,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}}=1
and

p_{s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}}=\frac{1}{10}
(as
v
is the desired successor) in the second
iteration of the repeat-loop on
Algorithms

3
to
3
.
In the last iteration,
p_{s,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}}
remains
1
and
p_{s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}}
changes to

\frac{9}{10}
as the desired successor changes to
r
. In both cases,
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}

is chosen by the goal-leaning
\arg\min
operator as
p_{s,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}}>p_{s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}}
.

Correctness.
We have only changed the behavior of the
\arg\min

operator when multiple candidates could be used. The correctness of our
algorithms does not depend on this choice and thus the proofs apply also to
the variant with the goal-leaning operator.

While the goal-leaning heuristic is simple, it has a great effect in practical
benchmarks; see
Section

IX
. However, there are scenarios where it
still fails. Consider now the CMDP in
Fig.

6
with capacity at
least
3
. Note that now
\gamma(s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}})
equals to
1
instead of
2
. In
this case, even the goal-leaning heuristic prefers
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}
in
s

whenever the current resource level is at least
1
. The reason for this
choice is that
\mathit{SV}(\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}},s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}})=1<2=\mathit{SV}(\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}},s,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}})
from the second iteration of the repeat-loop
onward.

s
t
u
r

]
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
[
0
]
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
[
1
]

\frac{1}{10}
\frac{9}{10}
[

]

.

Note also that the strategy
\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}}
that always plays

in

is not a witness strategy for
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{=1}
as

\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}}
needs at least

. The desired
strategy
\pi
should behave in
s
as follows: play
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}
if the current
resource is at least
2
and otherwise play
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}
. We have that

\mathit{ERT}(\pi,s,2,\{t\})=2
and
\mathit{ERT}(\pi,s,1,\{t\})=3.8
. In the next
section, we extend the goal-leaning heuristic to produce
\pi
for the
(updated) running example.

VIII-C

Threshold heuristic

The threshold heuristic is parametrized by a probability threshold
0\leq\theta\leq 1
. Intuitively, when we compute the value of
\mathit{SV}
for

{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}
in
s
, we ignore the hope values of successors
t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}})
such that
\Delta(s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}},t)<\theta
. With
\theta=0.2
,

v
in our example is no longer considered as a valid outcome for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}
in

s
in the second iteration and
\mathit{SV}(\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}},s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}})=\infty
.
Therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}
is picked with
\mathit{SV}(\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}},s,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}})=2
. It happens
only in the fourth iteration that action
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}
is considered from
s
. In
this iteration,
\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}}(r)
is
0
(it is a reload state) and
with
\Delta(s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}},r)=0.9
,
r
passes the threshold and we finally have
that
\mathit{SV}(\mathbf{p_{\mathit{old}}},s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}})=1
. The resulting finite counter strategy is
exactly the desired strategy
\pi
from above.

Formally, we parametrize the function
\mathit{SV}
by
\theta
as follows where
we assume
\min
of the empty set is equal to
\infty
(changes to definition
of
\mathit{SV}
are highlighted in
red
).

\mathit{SV}_{\!{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\theta}}(\mathbf{x},s,a)=\gamma(s,a)+\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{\prime}\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\Delta(s,a,s^{\prime})\geq\theta}\end{subarray}}\mathit{HV}(\mathbf{x},s,a,s^{\prime})

The new function
\mathit{SV}_{\!\theta}
is a generalization of
\mathit{SV}=\mathit{SV}_{\!0}
. To implement this heuristic, we need, in addition to the
goal-leaning
\arg\min
operator, to use
\mathit{SV}_{\!\theta}
instead of

\mathit{SV}
in
Algorithms

3
and
6
.

There is, however, still one caveat introduced by the threshold. By ignoring
some outcomes, the threshold heuristic might compute only over-approximations
of
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{>0}
. As a consequence, the strategy
\sigma

computed by the heuristic might be incomplete; it might be undefined for a
resource level from which the objective is still satisfiable.

In order to make
\sigma
complete and to compute
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{>0}

precisely, we continue with the iterations, but now using
\mathit{SV}_{\!0}

instead of
\mathit{SV}_{\!\theta}
. To be more precise, we include

Algorithms

3
to
3
in
Algorithm

3
twice
(and analogously for
Algorithm

6
), once with
\mathit{SV}_{\!\theta}
and
once with
\mathit{SV}_{\!0}
(in this order).

This extra fixed-point iteration can complete
\sigma
and improve
\mathbf{p}
to
match
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{>0}
using the rare outcomes ignored by the
threshold. As a result,
\sigma
behaves according to the threshold heuristic
for sufficiently high resource levels and, at the same time, it achieves the
objective from every state-level pair where this is possible.

Correctness.
The function
\mathit{SV}_{\!\theta}
clearly
over-approximates
\mathit{SV}
as we restrict the domain of the
\min
operator
only. The invariant of the repeat-loop from the proof of

Theorem

6
still holds even when using

\mathit{SV}_{\!\theta}
instead of
\mathit{SV}
(it also obviously holds in the
second loop with
\mathit{SV}_{\!0}
). The extra repeat-loop with
\mathit{SV}_{\!0}

converges to the correct fixed point due to the monotonicity of
\mathbf{p}
over
iterations. Thus,
Theorems

5
and
6
hold
even when using the threshold heuristics.

VIII-D

Limitations

The suggested heuristics naturally do not always produce strategies with the
least ERT possible for given CMDP, state, and initial load. Consider the CMDP
in
Fig.

7
with capacity at least
2
. Both heuristics prefer
(regardless
\theta
)
{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}}
in
s
since
\Delta(s,{\color[rgb]{0,0.8,0.8}\mathsf{b}},v)>\Delta(s,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}},v)=\Delta(s,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}},u)
. Such strategy yields ERT from
s

equal to
2\frac{2}{3}
, while the strategy that plays
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}\mathsf{a}}
in
s
comes
with ERT equal to
2
.

s
t
u
v
\mathsf{a}
\frac{1}{2}
\frac{1}{2}
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
\mathsf{b}
\frac{6}{10}
\frac{4}{10}
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
0
\mathsf{R}
=1
0
\mathsf{R}
=1
1
\mathsf{R}
=1
1
\mathsf{R}
=1

Fig. 7:
CMDP illustrating limitations of the goal-leaning heuristics. All
actions consume
1
unit of resource.

This non-optimality must be expected as the presented algorithm is purely
qualitative and does not convey any quantitative analysis that is required to
compute the precise ERT of strategies. However, there is no known polynomial
(with respect to the CMDP representation) algorithm for quantitative analysis
of CMDPs that we could use here instead of our approach.

While other, perhaps more involved heuristics might be invented to solve some
particular cases, qualitative algorithms which do not track precise values of
ERT, naturally cannot guarantee optimality with respect to ERT. The presented
heuristics are designed to be simple (both in principle and computation
overhead) and to work well on systems with rare undesired events.

The threshold heuristic relies on a well-chosen threshold
\theta
. This
threshold needs to be supplied by the user. Moreover, different thresholds
work well for different models. Typically,
\theta
should be chosen to be
higher than the probability of the most common rare events in the model, to
work well. As the presented algorithms rely on the fact that the whole model
is known, a suitable threshold might be automatically inferred from the model.

Despite these limitations, we show the utility of the presented heuristics on
a case study in the next section.

IX
Implementation and evaluation

We have implemented
Algorithms

1
to
6
,
including the proposed heuristics in a tool called
FiMDP
(Fuel in MDP). The
rest of this section presents two numerical examples that demonstrate utility
of
FiMDP
on realistic environments. In particular, we first compare the
speed of strategy synthesis via CMDPs performed by
FiMDP
to the speed of
strategy synthesis via regular MDPs with energy constraints encoded in states,
performed by
Storm
. The second example shows the impact of heuristics from

Section

VIII
on expected reachability time. Jupyter notebooks at

https://github.com/FiMDP/FiMDP-evaluation/tree/tac
contain (not only)
scripts and instructions needed to reproduce the presented results.

IX-A

Tools, examples, and evaluation setting

FiMDP
is an open-source library for CMDPs. It is written in Python and is
well integrated with interactive Jupyter notebooks
[
19
]
for
visualization of CMDPs and algorithms. With
Storm

[
1
]
and
Stormpy

installed,
FiMDP
can read models in PRISM
[
20
]
or JANI

[
21
]
languages.

Storm
is an open-source, state-of-the-art probabilistic model checker
designed to be efficient in terms of time and memory.
Storm
is written in
C++ and
Stormpy
is its Python interface. The examples are based on models
generated by
FiMDPEnv
— a library of simulation environments for real-world
resource-constrained problems that can be solved via CMDPs.

Table

I
lists the homepages of these tools and versions used to
create the presented results.

We demonstrate the utility of CMDPs and
FiMDP
on high-level planning tasks
for unmanned underwater vehicles (UUVs) operating in ocean with stochastic
currents.
FiMDPEnv
models this scenario based on
[

]
. The
model discretizes the area of interest into a 2D grid-world. A grid-world of
size
n
consists of
n\times n
cells, see
Fig.

8
(left). Each
cell in the grid-world forms one state in the corresponding CMDP, some of them
are reload states, and some of them form the set of targets
T
. The set
of actions consists of two classes of actions:
weak actions
consume
less energy but have stochastic outcomes whereas
strong actions
have
deterministic outcomes with the downside of significantly higher resource
consumption. For each class, the environment offers up to 8 directions (east,
north-east, north, north-west, west, south-west, south, and south-east), see
Fig.

8
(right).

IX-B

Strategy synthesis for CMDPs in
FiMDP
and

We use the UUV environment from

to generate 15 strategy synthesis
tasks with a Büchi objective. The complexity of a task is determined by grid
size (the number of cells on each side) and capacity. We use grid sizes
10
,

, and

. For each grid size
n
, we create five tasks with capacities
equal to
1
,

,

,

, and
10
times

.We solve each task modeled as a CMDP using
FiMDP
and modeled as a regular

Storm
. We
express the qualitative Büchi property in PCTL
[

]
for

.

Mean computation times for solving the CMDP model of the UUV environment with capacities proportional to the grid size in each task. Each subplot in the figure corresponds to a different size of the grid-world.

We can observe that
FiMDP
outperforms
Storm
in terms of computation time
in all test cases with the exception of small problems. For the small tasks,

Storm
benefits from its efficient implementation in C++. The advantage of

FiMDP
lies in the fact that the state space of CMDPs (and also the time
needed for their analysis) does not grow with rising capacity.

IX-C

Comparing heuristics for improving ERT

This section investigates the novel heuristics from a practical, optimal
decision-making perspective. The test scenario is based in the UUV environment
with grid size
20
, a single reload state and one target state. The objective
of agents is to reach the target almost surely. We consider four strategies
generated for almost-sure reachability by the presented algorithms: the
standard strategy (using randomized
\arg\min
operator), and strategies
generated using the goal-leaning
\arg\min
operator and thresholds
\theta

equal to
0
,
0.3
, and
0.5
. For each strategy, we run
10000
independent
runs and measure the number of steps needed to reach the target. By averaging
the collected data, we approximate the expected reachability time (ERT) for
the strategies.

TABLE II:
Approximated ERT for strategies with standard and goal-leaning
\arg\min
operators

operator

\mathbf{\theta}

ERT

standard

—

200
+

goal-leaning

0\hphantom{.2}

51.27

goal-leaning

0.3

19.53

goal-leaning

0.5

15.00

Table

II
shows the average number of steps needed to reach the
target by each of the strategies. The strategy built using the standard

\arg\min
operator does not reach the target within the first
200
steps in
any of the
10000
trials. The goal-leaning
\arg\min
operator itself helps a
lot to navigate the agent towards the goal. However, it still relies on rare
events at some places. Setting
\theta=0.3
helps to avoid these situations as
the unlikely outcomes are not considered any more, and finally
\theta=0.5

forces the agent to use strong actions almost exclusively. While using
thresholds led to a better ERT in this particular environment, the result
might not hold in general. The best choice of threshold solely depends on the
environment model and the exact probabilities of outcomes.

X
Conclusion & future work

We presented consumption Markov decision processes — models for stochastic
environments with resource constraints — and we showed that strategy
synthesis for qualitative objectives is efficient. In particular, our
algorithms that solve synthesis for almost-sure reachability and almost-sure
Büchi objective in CMDPs, work in time polynomial with respect to the
representation of the input CMDP. In addition, we presented two heuristics
that can significantly improve the expected time needed to reach a target in
realistic examples. These heuristics improve the utility of the presented
algorithms for planning in stochastic environments under resource constraints.
The experimental evaluation of the suggested methods confirmed that direct
analysis of CMDPs in out tool is faster than analysis of an equivalent MDP
even when performed by the state-of-the-art tool
Storm
(with the exception of
very small models).

Possible directions for the future work include extensions to quantitative
analysis (e.g. minimizing the expected resource consumption or reachability
time), stochastic games, or partially observable setting.

\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T}))(s)\leq\gamma(s,a)+\max_{t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)}d_{t}\leq\mathit{cap}
(from the definition of

\mathit{RL}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-13.79431pt{d}\kern 11.13281pt}}_{{\kern-19.40515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.13281pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-13.79431pt{d}\kern 11.13281pt}}_{{\kern-19.40515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.13281pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-8.5892pt{d}\kern 6.65639pt}}_{{\kern-12.59694pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.65639pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-6.68738pt{d}\kern 4.75456pt}}_{{\kern-10.69511pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.75456pt}}})
). If
s\in\mathit{R}
, then it follows that
b=\llbracket\,\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T}))\,\rrbracket_{\mathit{R}}^{\mathit{cap}}(s)=0\leq d
. If
s\not\in\mathit{R}
, then
b=\llbracket\,\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T}))\,\rrbracket_{\mathit{R}}^{\mathit{cap}}(s)=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T}))(s)\leq\gamma(s,a)+\max_{t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)}d_{t}\leq d
, the first inequality shown above and the second coming from (
2
).

Let us first prove (1.). We denote by
\tau
the strategy such that
for all histories
\alpha
we have
\tau(\alpha)=\sigma(sa\alpha)
. For each

t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)
, we have
\tau\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{t}}}}_{{{t}}}}\models^{{\kern-11.95161pt{d_{t}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-9.40678pt{t}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{t}}}}_{{{t}}}}\models^{{\kern-11.95161pt{d_{t}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-9.40678pt{t}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{t}}}}_{{{t}}}}\models^{{\kern-9.64792pt{d_{t}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-7.32622pt{t}\kern 5.95122pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{t}}}}_{{{t}}}}\models^{{\kern-8.59793pt{d_{t}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-6.27623pt{t}\kern 4.90123pt{}}}}\!\mathsf{S}
. Moreover, there
exists
q\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)
such that

\tau\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{q}}}}_{{{q}}}}\models^{{\kern-12.37573pt{d_{q}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-10.00056pt{q}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{q}}}}_{{{q}}}}\models^{{\kern-12.37573pt{d_{q}}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-10.00056pt{q}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{q}}}}_{{{q}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.07204pt{d_{q}}\kern 5.95122pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-7.75034pt{q}\kern 5.95122pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d_{q}}}}_{{{q}}}}\models^{{\kern-9.02205pt{d_{q}}\kern 4.90123pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-6.70035pt{q}\kern 4.90123pt{}}}}\!\mathsf{R}^{i}
(since
s\notin T
); hence,
by induction hypothesis it holds
\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T})(q)\leq d_{q}
. From this and from the definition of
\mathit{SV}
we get

\displaystyle\mathit{SV}(\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T}),s,a)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma(s,a)+\mathit{HV}(\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T}),s,a,q)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma(s,a)+\!\!\!\!\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)\\
t\neq q\end{subarray}}\!\!\{\mathcal{B}^{i}(\mathbf{y}_{T})(q),\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{S}](s)\}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma(s,a)+\max_{t\in\mathit{Succ}(s,a)}d_{t}.

To finish, (
2
) follows immediately from the definition
of
d_{t}
and the fact that
\mathit{last}\mathit{RL}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-13.79431pt{d}\kern 11.13281pt}}_{{\kern-19.40515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.13281pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-13.79431pt{d}\kern 11.13281pt}}_{{\kern-19.40515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 11.13281pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-8.5892pt{d}\kern 6.65639pt}}_{{\kern-12.59694pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.65639pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}sat^{{\kern-6.68738pt{d}\kern 4.75456pt}}_{{\kern-10.69511pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.75456pt}}})
is always bounded from above by

d-\gamma(s,a)
for
s\notin\mathit{R}
.
∎

-B

Proof of
Lemma

4

Proof of
Lemma

4
.

By
Lemma

3
, it suffices to show that
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{>0}=\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}^{K}]^{>0}
. To show this, fix any
state
s
such that
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{>0}(s)<\infty
. For the
sake of succinctness, we denote
\mathbf{ml}[\mathsf{R}]^{>0}(s)
by

d
. To each strategy
\pi
such that
\pi\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.88403pt{d}\kern 5.95122pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-7.70296pt{s}\kern 5.95122pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-6.83405pt{d}\kern 4.90123pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-6.65297pt{s}\kern 4.90123pt{}}}}\!\mathsf{R}

we assign a
reachability index
that is the infimum of all
i
such that

\pi\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.88403pt{d}\kern 5.95122pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-7.70296pt{s}\kern 5.95122pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-6.83405pt{d}\kern 4.90123pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-6.65297pt{s}\kern 4.90123pt{}}}}\!\mathsf{R}^{i}
. Let
\sigma
be a strategy such
that
\sigma\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.88403pt{d}\kern 5.95122pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-7.70296pt{s}\kern 5.95122pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-6.83405pt{d}\kern 4.90123pt{\!>0}}}_{{\kern-6.65297pt{s}\kern 4.90123pt{}}}}\!\mathsf{R}
with the minimal reachability index
k
.
We show that the
k\leq K
.

We proceed by a suitable “strategy surgery” on
\sigma
. Let

\alpha
be a history produced by
\sigma
from
s
of length
k

whose last state belongs to
T
. Assume, for the sake of
contradiction, that
k>K
. This can only be if at least one of the
following conditions hold:

(a)

Some reload state is visited twice on
\alpha
, i.e. there are
0\leq j<l\leq k+1
such that
\alpha_{l}=\alpha_{j}\in\mathit{R}
, or

(b)

some state is visited twice with no intermediate visits to a
reload state; i.e., there are
0\leq j<l\leq k+1
such that
\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{l}
and
\alpha_{h}\not\in\mathit{R}
for all
j<h<l
.

Indeed, if none of the conditions hold, then the reload states
partition
\alpha
into at most
|\mathit{R}|+1
segments, each
segment containing non-reload states without repetition. This would
imply
k=len(\alpha)\leq K
.

In both cases (a) and (b) we can arrive at a contradiction using essentially
the same argument. Let us illustrate the details on case (a): Consider a
strategy
\pi
such that for every history of the form
\alpha_{..j}\odot\beta
for a suitable
\beta
we have
\pi(\alpha_{..j}\odot\beta)=\sigma(\alpha_{..l}\odot\beta)
; on all other
histories,
\pi
mimics
\sigma
. Clearly
\pi\!\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-10.18773pt{d}\kern 7.52623pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-9.9342pt{s}\kern 7.52623pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-7.88403pt{d}\kern 5.95122pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-7.70296pt{s}\kern 5.95122pt{}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{{s}}}}\models^{{\kern-6.83405pt{d}\kern 4.90123pt{\!}}}_{{\kern-6.65297pt{s}\kern 4.90123pt{}}}}\!\mathsf{S}
:
the behavior changed only for histories with
\alpha_{..j}
as a prefix and
for each suitable
\beta
we have

\mathit{last}\mathit{RL}(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha_{..j}\odot\beta^{{\kern-25.98624pt{d}\kern 23.32474pt}}_{{\kern-31.59708pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 23.32474pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha_{..j}\odot\beta^{{\kern-25.98624pt{d}\kern 23.32474pt}}_{{\kern-31.59708pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 23.32474pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha_{..j}\odot\beta^{{\kern-17.5571pt{d}\kern 15.62428pt}}_{{\kern-21.56483pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 15.62428pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha_{..j}\odot\beta^{{\kern-14.78076pt{d}\kern 12.84795pt}}_{{\kern-18.7885pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 22.58487pt}}_{{\kern-30.85721pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 22.58487pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha_{..l}\odot\beta^{{\kern-25.24637pt{d}\kern 22.58487pt}}_{{\kern-30.85721pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 22.58487pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha_{..l}\odot\beta^{{\kern-17.02863pt{d}\kern 12.31947pt}}})
due to the fact that

\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{l}
is a reload state. Moreover, we have
that
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-7.86026pt{d}\kern 5.19876pt}}_{{\kern-13.4711pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.19876pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-5.06735pt{d}\kern 3.13454pt}}_{{\kern-9.07509pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.13454pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\alpha^{{\kern-4.17177pt{d}\kern 2.23895pt}}_{{\kern-8.1795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.23895pt}}}_{..j}\odot\alpha_{l..k}
created by

\pi
reaches
T
in
k^{\prime}=k-(l-j)<k
steps which is the
reachability index of
\pi
. We reached a contradiction with the choice of

\sigma
.

For case (b), the only difference is that now the resource level after

\alpha_{..j}\odot\beta
can be higher then the one of

\alpha_{..l}\odot\beta
due to the removal of the intermediate
non-reloading cycle. Since we need to show that the energy level never
drops below 0, the same argument works.
∎
